English for Work · Industry & infrastructure

Housekeeping and Commercial Cleaning English

Confirm assigned cleaning work, respect occupied spaces, clarify product information, report building concerns, manage supplies, and give accurate completion updates.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book

Words into action

For Housekeeping attendants, commercial cleaners, custodial staff, and cleaning team members.

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Restate a defined cleaning assignment, clarify an extra request, and preserve authorization despite a deadline.
  • Acknowledge a declined visit, offer the actual return window, and distinguish an agreed plan from room-entry permission.
  • Report uncertain product identity, request understandable information, and resist identification by color or familiarity.
  • Give an exact location and observed condition, preserve uncertainty about the cause, and avoid premature clearance.
  • Describe found property without inspecting private contents, record its location, and confirm transfer to the designated receiver.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All sites, rooms, quantities, guests, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not chemical handling, equipment operation, hazard control, legal advice, or specialist infection prevention. Follow actual training, product labels and safety data sheets, site access rules, privacy and found-property procedures, and emergency arrangements. No example authorizes unsafe access, use of an unidentified product, or release of an unchecked area.

01Confirming the assignment and its limitsWhat does everything include?+

Your goal

Restate a defined cleaning assignment, clarify an extra request, and preserve authorization despite a deadline.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

What does everything include?

Cleaner Maya has a commercial assignment reading conference room: tables and floor. It covers table surfaces and the floor, not high windows or personal equipment. Client Jordan asks for everything before a 3:00 meeting and points to the high windows and laptops on the tables. Supervisor Rosa can clarify and authorize any additional work through the site process. No extra work has been approved, and the deadline does not expand permission. Maya must acknowledge the meeting, name the actual scope, and arrange clarification without promising all requested tasks.

Jordan: Client contact / Maya: Cleaner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is Maya assigned to clean?

Answer and explanation

C. Table surfaces and the floor

The assignment specifically covers table surfaces and the floor, with stated exclusions.

2. Who can clarify extra work?

Answer and explanation

D. Supervisor Rosa

Rosa is identified as the supervisor who can handle changes through the site process.

3. What does the 3:00 meeting establish?

Answer and explanation

C. A stated time constraint, not expanded permission

The meeting time describes urgency but does not change the authorized scope.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Before three identifies the requested deadline. It does not mean all tasks are authorized or feasible. Preserve the difference between the current assignment, an additional request, and an approved change.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
assignment
Work specifically allocated to a worker or team.confirm the assignment
scope of work
Defined tasks and boundaries of an assignment.clarify the scope of work
task list
Written list of required work items.review the task list
table surface
Exposed working area of a table.clean the table surfaces
floor area
Specified section of floor within the assignment.identify the floor area
high window
Window area requiring access above ordinary floor-level work.clarify high-window work
personal equipment
Devices or items belonging to individual users.exclude personal equipment
client contact
Person communicating requirements for the customer organization.speak with the client contact
site supervisor
Person overseeing assigned work at the location.consult the site supervisor
additional work
Work beyond the current assignment.request additional work
authorization
Actual permission from the relevant responsible person or process.obtain authorization
work order
Record describing requested or assigned work.check the work order
service specification
Description of the service and required outcome.follow the service specification
exclusion
Item or task explicitly outside an assignment.explain an exclusion
deadline
Time by which a task is requested or required.clarify the deadline
meeting setup
Arrangement of a room for a meeting.separate cleaning from meeting setup
surface access
Ability and permission to reach the relevant surface.confirm surface access
approved change
Change accepted through the required process.record an approved change
priority
Relative importance or order of assigned tasks.confirm the priority
capacity
Available time, resources, and ability for the work.check team capacity
completion estimate
Approximate expected finishing time.obtain a completion estimate
cleanable area
Measured area included in the specified cleaning task, which may differ from total building area.measure the cleanable area
labor-hour
One hour of work by one person, distinct from elapsed clock time for a crew.calculate labor-hours
read-back
Repetition of the understood instruction for confirmation.give a scope read-back

Tables can mean table surfaces rather than every object resting on them. If access is blocked by personal equipment, ask for the actual site arrangement. Do not infer permission to handle devices or belongings from a general cleaning request.

C Notice the language

A boundary can include a next step

A direct request from a client may feel difficult to question. Acknowledge the practical need, state the assigned work plainly, and name who can clarify an addition. Helpful language does not require agreeing to work outside the actual authorization or training.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the timing
I understand the meeting starts at three.
Read the assignment
My task list says table surfaces and the floor.
Clarify everything
Which additional areas do you mean by everything?
Name the exclusion
High windows are not in my current assignment.
Protect personal equipment
Personal devices are outside the stated scope.
Offer the next step
I can ask Rosa to clarify the extra request.
Keep approval distinct
That request has not been approved yet.
Avoid a blanket promise
I cannot confirm all of that from this assignment.
Check the boundary
Does the work order include that surface?
Explain the timing
I understand the deadline; I still need approval for the extra tasks.
Ask about access
What is the approved arrangement for clearing the table surfaces?
Keep responsibility clear
Rosa can confirm whether we can add that work.
Summarize the present scope
Table surfaces and floor remain the assigned tasks.
Avoid a guessed estimate
I will not give a completion time I have not checked.
Report the outcome
I will pass on the clarified instruction.
Close the read-back
I understand the request; I am checking the change before agreeing to it.
6 language and precision notes
Covers
Covers defines included work without implying every nearby task is included.
Outside the scope
This phrase states a task boundary, not a personal unwillingness to help.
Before versus by
Before three means earlier than three; by three means no later than three. Neither authorizes extra work.
Request versus approval
A person can request more work before the authorized process accepts it.
Everything
Replace this broad word with specific surfaces and tasks.
Can ask
This commits to seeking clarification, not to receiving a particular answer.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response is clearest?

Answer and explanation

C. My assignment covers the tables and floor; I will check the extra work with Rosa.

The response preserves scope and gives a practical route for the additional request.

2. Which statement wrongly treats urgency as permission?

Answer and explanation

D. The meeting is at three, so high windows are now authorized.

A time constraint does not authorize an excluded task or change the assignment.

3. What should Maya avoid promising?

Answer and explanation

B. Completion of every requested extra before three

No approval or feasibility check supports a promise to complete all additional work.

4. Why clarify tables?

Answer and explanation

A. Objects on a table may be outside the assigned surface-cleaning scope.

Table-surface work does not itself authorize handling personal devices resting on the table.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

What does everything include?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jordan: Client contact / Maya: Cleaner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • clarification request
  • task list
  • site supervisor
  • additional work
  • scope of work
  • assignment
  • surface access
  • read-back
  • deadline
  • approved change
  1. Jordan
    We have a meeting at three, so could you do everything in the conference room? The high windows and the laptops need attention too.
  2. Maya
    I understand the timing. My 1 covers the table surfaces and the floor. I need to clarify the extra areas before agreeing to include them.
  3. Jordan
    I thought the laptops were included because they are on the tables. Can you wipe those too?
  4. Maya
    The 2 names the surfaces, not the personal equipment on them. I do not have permission from this task list to clean those devices.
  5. Jordan
    What about the high windows? They are the first thing people see from the meeting table.
  6. Maya
    They would be 3. I can ask Rosa about the request, but I cannot treat the meeting time as approval to take on that task.
  7. Jordan
    Who is Rosa in relation to this job? I would like the request to reach someone who can give us a clear answer.
  8. Maya
    Rosa is the 4. She can clarify the authorized work and the arrangements needed for anything beyond my present assignment.
  9. Jordan
    Please ask her. I need to tell the organizer what we can realistically expect.
  10. Maya
    I will raise a 5 identifying the high windows and personal equipment. I will also mention the three o'clock meeting as the time constraint.
  11. Jordan
    While you check, which tasks can I say are definitely in the current assignment?
  12. Maya
    You can say the 6 covers table surfaces and floor. The extra request is being checked; no change has been approved yet.
  13. Jordan
    Some laptops are blocking the table surfaces. Are you allowed to move them, or should their owners deal with that?
  14. Maya
    Please do not assume that. We need the approved 7 arrangement for those areas. Cleaning the table does not automatically authorize me to handle personal equipment.
  15. Jordan
    Understood. I can wait for the clarification on that. The main thing is to avoid different people giving different instructions at once.
  16. Maya
    Yes. Any 8 needs to be clear in the work instructions. That helps the team know what is included and what remains outside the task.
  17. Jordan
    So I should not tell everyone the extra work will be finished by three?
  18. Maya
    Correct. The 9 is noted, but I have not confirmed extra work or a completion estimate for it. Rosa needs to clarify those points.
  19. Jordan
    Understood. Please ask about the windows, the laptops, and the three o'clock deadline.
  20. Maya
    That is an accurate 10. I will take the clarification to Rosa and communicate the actual outcome, without treating the request itself as permission to proceed.

Answers and explanations
  1. assignment Turn 2. Assignment identifies the work currently allocated: table surfaces and floor, not every requested addition.
  2. scope of work Turn 4. Scope of work defines the included tasks and boundaries; table surfaces do not automatically include personal equipment.
  3. additional work Turn 6. Additional work is outside the current assignment and needs clarification rather than automatic inclusion because of urgency.
  4. site supervisor Turn 8. The site supervisor is the identified person who can clarify and authorize changes through the actual site process.
  5. clarification request Turn 10. A clarification request seeks a defined decision about the extra tasks without implying the answer is already yes.
  6. task list Turn 12. The task list provides the confirmed included work and should not be enlarged in a message to another person.
  7. surface access Turn 14. Surface access includes the practical and authorized arrangement for reaching the table without assuming permission to handle belongings.
  8. approved change Turn 16. An approved change is a modification actually accepted through the relevant process, unlike the still-pending request.
  9. deadline Turn 18. Deadline names the time requirement but does not establish authorization, resources, or a reliable completion promise.
  10. read-back Turn 20. Read-back repeats the confirmed scope and pending request, checking that both people share the same understanding.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jordan: We have a meeting at three, so could you do everything in the conference room? The high windows and the laptops need attention too.
  2. Maya: I understand the timing. My assignment covers the table surfaces and the floor. I need to clarify the extra areas before agreeing to include them.
  3. Jordan: I thought the laptops were included because they are on the tables. Can you wipe those too?
  4. Maya: The scope of work names the surfaces, not the personal equipment on them. I do not have permission from this task list to clean those devices.
  5. Jordan: What about the high windows? They are the first thing people see from the meeting table.
  6. Maya: They would be additional work. I can ask Rosa about the request, but I cannot treat the meeting time as approval to take on that task.
  7. Jordan: Who is Rosa in relation to this job? I would like the request to reach someone who can give us a clear answer.
  8. Maya: Rosa is the site supervisor. She can clarify the authorized work and the arrangements needed for anything beyond my present assignment.
  9. Jordan: Please ask her. I need to tell the organizer what we can realistically expect.
  10. Maya: I will raise a clarification request identifying the high windows and personal equipment. I will also mention the three o'clock meeting as the time constraint.
  11. Jordan: While you check, which tasks can I say are definitely in the current assignment?
  12. Maya: You can say the task list covers table surfaces and floor. The extra request is being checked; no change has been approved yet.
  13. Jordan: Some laptops are blocking the table surfaces. Are you allowed to move them, or should their owners deal with that?
  14. Maya: Please do not assume that. We need the approved surface access arrangement for those areas. Cleaning the table does not automatically authorize me to handle personal equipment.
  15. Jordan: Understood. I can wait for the clarification on that. The main thing is to avoid different people giving different instructions at once.
  16. Maya: Yes. Any approved change needs to be clear in the work instructions. That helps the team know what is included and what remains outside the task.
  17. Jordan: So I should not tell everyone the extra work will be finished by three?
  18. Maya: Correct. The deadline is noted, but I have not confirmed extra work or a completion estimate for it. Rosa needs to clarify those points.
  19. Jordan: Understood. Please ask about the windows, the laptops, and the three o'clock deadline.
  20. Maya: That is an accurate read-back. I will take the clarification to Rosa and communicate the actual outcome, without treating the request itself as permission to proceed.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Everything has a boundary

The conference-room assignment in the case.

  1. Client: Could you clean everything in this conference room before our three o'clock meeting?
  2. Cleaner: My assignment sheet lists tables and floor. Which other items did you mean?
  3. Client: The high windows look marked, and there are laptops on some of the tables.
  4. Cleaner: Those aren't included in my task list. I can ask my supervisor about the additional request.
  5. Client: Please put the tables and floor first. The meeting can't move to another room.
  6. Cleaner: I can prioritize that assigned work, but I won't handle laptops without the site's instructions.
  7. Client: We'll ask their owners to clear them. Can you report any table area you couldn't reach?
  8. Cleaner: Yes. I'll describe any inaccessible surfaces rather than mark the whole room complete.
  9. Client: And please ask about the windows without promising they'll be finished before the meeting.
  10. Cleaner: I will. The listed table and floor work remains my assignment; the windows need review.
A spot or the whole carpet?

A cleaner clarifies a client's request about a marked carpet.

  1. Cleaner: You asked me to address the carpet. Do you mean this patch or the entire room?
  2. Client: This small patch beside the door is the issue. The rest is on the usual schedule.
  3. Cleaner: Thank you. I'll report a spot-cleaning request for that patch, not a whole-room treatment.
  4. Client: Can you guarantee the mark will disappear before the visitors arrive this afternoon?
  5. Cleaner: I can't promise that outcome. I'll ask the supervisor about the suitable work and timing.
  6. Client: Please tell them it's near the entrance, where visitors will see it first.
  7. Cleaner: I'll give the exact location and note that you haven't requested the whole carpet today.
  8. Client: That's right. I want the particular mark assessed without changing the full cleaning schedule.
  9. Cleaner: Is the visitor arrival time two o'clock, as shown on the room booking?
  10. Client: Yes, two o'clock. Please use that time when you discuss the request with your supervisor.
Touchpoints in the corridor

A team member clarifies the location of a daily task.

  1. Cleaner: The sheet says corridor touchpoints. Does that include the handles inside the private offices?
  2. Team lead: Today's assignment covers shared corridor handles and railings, not entry into the private offices.
  3. Cleaner: Thanks. I wanted to check the boundary before assuming the office doors were included.
  4. Team lead: The shared-side handles are included, but office access is a separate arrangement for another shift.
  5. Cleaner: I'll describe the task as the shared corridor touchpoints, with no private-office entry.
  6. Team lead: Exactly. Please also identify which corridor section you're covering so we don't duplicate the work.
  7. Cleaner: I'll take the east corridor between the lifts and the meeting-room entrance this shift.
  8. Team lead: Then I'll assign the west corridor to Noor and leave the private-office list separate.
  9. Cleaner: If a tenant asks me to add an office, I'll refer that extra request to you.
  10. Team lead: Please do. We can clarify access and scope before changing either worker's assignment.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What does everything include?
  1. Read the scope conversation, emphasizing tables and floor versus windows and devices.
  2. Repeat Maya's request to Rosa with both extra tasks and the meeting deadline.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify a different commercial assignment

A work order covers reception counters and the lobby floor. The client adds exterior signs before noon. Supervisor Leon can review changes, but no extra work is approved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Cleaner: "The assigned horizontal surfaces are the reception ___."

Answer and explanation

C. counters

Counters are the named surfaces included in this work order.

2. Client: "The additional request concerns the exterior ___."

Answer and explanation

C. signs

Exterior signs are the requested addition, not an already included task.

3. Cleaner: "I will ask ___ to review the request."

Answer and explanation

B. Leon

Leon is the supervisor identified for reviewing any proposed change.

4. Cleaner: "The extra work is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. approved

The brief explicitly states that approval has not been given.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Confirming the assignment and its limits
Original fictional case: Cleaner Maya has a commercial assignment reading conference room: tables and floor. It covers table surfaces and the floor, not high windows or personal equipment. Client Jordan asks for everything before a 3:00 meeting and points to the high windows and laptops on the tables. Supervisor Rosa can clarify and authorize any additional work through the site process. No extra work has been approved, and the deadline does not expand permission. Maya must acknowledge the meeting, name the actual scope, and arrange clarification without promising all requested tasks.
Language workshop: What does everything include?
Communication goal: Restate a defined cleaning assignment, clarify an extra request, and preserve authorization despite a deadline.
Language guidance: Before three identifies the requested deadline. It does not mean all tasks are authorized or feasible. Preserve the difference between the current assignment, an additional request, and an approved change.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand the meeting starts at three. / My task list says table surfaces and the floor. / Which additional areas do you mean by everything? / High windows are not in my current assignment. / Personal devices are outside the stated scope. / I can ask Rosa to clarify the extra request. / That request has not been approved yet. / I cannot confirm all of that from this assignment. / Does the work order include that surface? / I understand the deadline; I still need approval for the extra tasks. / What is the approved arrangement for clearing the table surfaces? / Rosa can confirm whether we can add that work. / Table surfaces and floor remain the assigned tasks. / I will not give a completion time I have not checked. / I will pass on the clarified instruction. / I understand the request; I am checking the change before agreeing to it.
Vocabulary: assignment: Work specifically allocated to a worker or team. / scope of work: Defined tasks and boundaries of an assignment. / task list: Written list of required work items. / table surface: Exposed working area of a table. / floor area: Specified section of floor within the assignment. / high window: Window area requiring access above ordinary floor-level work. / personal equipment: Devices or items belonging to individual users. / client contact: Person communicating requirements for the customer organization. / site supervisor: Person overseeing assigned work at the location. / additional work: Work beyond the current assignment. / authorization: Actual permission from the relevant responsible person or process. / work order: Record describing requested or assigned work. / service specification: Description of the service and required outcome. / exclusion: Item or task explicitly outside an assignment. / deadline: Time by which a task is requested or required. / meeting setup: Arrangement of a room for a meeting. / surface access: Ability and permission to reach the relevant surface. / approved change: Change accepted through the required process. / priority: Relative importance or order of assigned tasks. / capacity: Available time, resources, and ability for the work. / completion estimate: Approximate expected finishing time. / cleanable area: Measured area included in the specified cleaning task, which may differ from total building area. / labor-hour: One hour of work by one person, distinct from elapsed clock time for a crew. / read-back: Repetition of the understood instruction for confirmation.
Speaking task: Read the scope conversation, emphasizing tables and floor versus windows and devices. Repeat Maya's request to Rosa with both extra tasks and the meeting deadline. Complete the reception scenario without treating the exterior signs as approved work.
Partner: Jordan: Client contact / Maya: Cleaner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A work order covers reception counters and the lobby floor. The client adds exterior signs before noon. Supervisor Leon can review changes, but no extra work is approved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jordan: So I should not tell everyone the extra work will be finished by three?
Maya: Correct. The deadline is noted, but I have not confirmed extra work or a completion estimate for it. Rosa needs to clarify those points.
Jordan: Understood. Please ask about the windows, the laptops, and the three o'clock deadline.
Maya: That is an accurate read-back. I will take the clarification to Rosa and communicate the actual outcome, without treating the request itself as permission to proceed.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Communication goal: Restate a defined cleaning assignment, clarify an extra request, and preserve authorization despite a deadline.
Language guidance: Before three identifies the requested deadline. It does not mean all tasks are authorized or feasible. Preserve the difference between the current assignment, an additional request, and an approved change.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand the meeting starts at three. / My task list says table surfaces and the floor. / Which additional areas do you mean by everything? / High windows are not in my current assignment. / Personal devices are outside the stated scope. / I can ask Rosa to clarify the extra request. / That request has not been approved yet. / I cannot confirm all of that from this assignment. / Does the work order include that surface? / I understand the deadline; I still need approval for the extra tasks. / What is the approved arrangement for clearing the table surfaces? / Rosa can confirm whether we can add that work. / Table surfaces and floor remain the assigned tasks. / I will not give a completion time I have not checked. / I will pass on the clarified instruction. / I understand the request; I am checking the change before agreeing to it.
Vocabulary: assignment: Work specifically allocated to a worker or team. / scope of work: Defined tasks and boundaries of an assignment. / task list: Written list of required work items. / table surface: Exposed working area of a table. / floor area: Specified section of floor within the assignment. / high window: Window area requiring access above ordinary floor-level work. / personal equipment: Devices or items belonging to individual users. / client contact: Person communicating requirements for the customer organization. / site supervisor: Person overseeing assigned work at the location. / additional work: Work beyond the current assignment. / authorization: Actual permission from the relevant responsible person or process. / work order: Record describing requested or assigned work. / service specification: Description of the service and required outcome. / exclusion: Item or task explicitly outside an assignment. / deadline: Time by which a task is requested or required. / meeting setup: Arrangement of a room for a meeting. / surface access: Ability and permission to reach the relevant surface. / approved change: Change accepted through the required process. / priority: Relative importance or order of assigned tasks. / capacity: Available time, resources, and ability for the work. / completion estimate: Approximate expected finishing time. / cleanable area: Measured area included in the specified cleaning task, which may differ from total building area. / labor-hour: One hour of work by one person, distinct from elapsed clock time for a crew. / read-back: Repetition of the understood instruction for confirmation.
Speaking task: Read the scope conversation, emphasizing tables and floor versus windows and devices. Repeat Maya's request to Rosa with both extra tasks and the meeting deadline. Complete the reception scenario without treating the exterior signs as approved work.
Partner: Jordan: Client contact / Maya: Cleaner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A work order covers reception counters and the lobby floor. The client adds exterior signs before noon. Supervisor Leon can review changes, but no extra work is approved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jordan: So I should not tell everyone the extra work will be finished by three?
Maya: Correct. The deadline is noted, but I have not confirmed extra work or a completion estimate for it. Rosa needs to clarify those points.
Jordan: Understood. Please ask about the windows, the laptops, and the three o'clock deadline.
Maya: That is an accurate read-back. I will take the clarification to Rosa and communicate the actual outcome, without treating the request itself as permission to proceed.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Confirming the assignment and its limits
Original fictional case: Cleaner Maya has a commercial assignment reading conference room: tables and floor. It covers table surfaces and the floor, not high windows or personal equipment. Client Jordan asks for everything before a 3:00 meeting and points to the high windows and laptops on the tables. Supervisor Rosa can clarify and authorize any additional work through the site process. No extra work has been approved, and the deadline does not expand permission. Maya must acknowledge the meeting, name the actual scope, and arrange clarification without promising all requested tasks.
Language workshop: What does everything include?
Communication goal: Restate a defined cleaning assignment, clarify an extra request, and preserve authorization despite a deadline.

02Respecting access and occupied spacesNot during the call+

Your goal

Acknowledge a declined visit, offer the actual return window, and distinguish an agreed plan from room-entry permission.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Not during the call

At 10:00, guest Alex in room 312 declines cleaning because a call is expected to last until 11:00. Attendant Lina can return between 11:15 and 11:30 or offer fresh towels at the door now. Alex chooses the return window and does not request towels at the door. Lina must record that plan accurately without promising exactly 11:00 or treating the end of the call as permission to enter. Actual room-access procedures still apply when the attendant returns. No room entry or cleaning completion occurs in this conversation.

Alex: Guest in room 312 / Lina: Room attendant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why is cleaning declined at 10:00?

Answer and explanation

A. The guest is on a call until about 11:00

The stated reason is the call, not a permanent cancellation of service.

2. What return window is available?

Answer and explanation

B. 11:15-11:30

Lina's actual available return window begins at eleven fifteen and ends at eleven thirty.

3. What does Alex choose?

Answer and explanation

C. The return window, without towels now

Alex chooses the later offered window and declines the separate towel option.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Until eleven describes the guest call. Between eleven fifteen and eleven thirty describes the available return window. Neither means the attendant has promised to arrive exactly when the call ends.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
occupied room
Room currently in use by a guest or other occupant.respect an occupied room
room attendant
Worker assigned to guest-room cleaning and related service.speak with the room attendant
declined service
Offered service the occupant does not accept at that time.record declined service
return window
Time range in which a worker can come back.offer a return window
appointment time
Specific agreed time rather than a broad window.confirm the appointment time
privacy
Freedom from inappropriate intrusion or disclosure.respect guest privacy
access permission
Authorization to enter or use a particular space.confirm access permission
entry procedure
Actual required steps and rules for entering a space.follow the entry procedure
doorstep delivery
Handover of an item at the doorway rather than room entry.offer doorstep delivery
fresh towels
Clean towels available for guest use.offer fresh towels
service option
Available choice about the service to be provided.explain a service option
do-not-disturb notice
Guest instruction or signal requesting no interruption under site rules.respect a do-not-disturb notice
service request
Guest's stated request for an available service.record a service request
visit plan
Arrangement for a future visit.confirm the visit plan
availability
Time or circumstances in which a person or service can be accessed.clarify availability
time range
Interval with a beginning and end.repeat the time range
scheduled return
Planned later visit within the agreed arrangement.record a scheduled return
unoccupied
Not currently occupied, where that status is actually established.verify unoccupied status
guest preference
Stated service choice or wish of the guest.record the guest preference
access record
Record of relevant entry or permission information.update the access record
interruption
Action that disturbs an ongoing activity.avoid an unnecessary interruption
service deferral
Moving a service to a later available opportunity.arrange a service deferral
exact-time promise
Commitment to a specific arrival time.avoid an unsupported exact-time promise
confirmation
Clear agreement about the understood plan.obtain confirmation

A later visit plan does not replace actual access rules. Do not equate silence, an expected end time, or a prior conversation with permission to enter. Keep room status, guest preference, and entry authorization distinct.

C Notice the language

Give control without inventing availability

A guest may need privacy without wanting to cancel service entirely. Offer the real options in plain language and repeat the chosen window. Respecting the current refusal is compatible with arranging a later visit; neither requires assuming future entry permission.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the refusal
Certainly; I will not start service now.
Clarify the timing
Your call is expected to finish at eleven.
Offer the real window
I can return between eleven fifteen and eleven thirty.
Avoid an exact promise
Eleven is not available, but eleven fifteen to eleven thirty is.
Offer the separate option
I can offer fresh towels at the door now.
Offer doorstep service
I can hand the towels to you at the door.
Ask for the choice
Which of those options would suit you?
Confirm the return
I have recorded the eleven-fifteen-to-eleven-thirty window.
Preserve the refusal
Cleaning was declined at ten because of your call.
Avoid overgeneralizing
You are postponing this visit, not cancelling every service.
Respect privacy
I understand that you do not want an interruption.
Explain the later check
We will still follow the entry procedure when we return.
Keep towels distinct
No towel delivery is requested now.
Repeat the room
This plan is for room 312.
Check agreement
Have I understood your preference correctly?
Close politely
Thank you; I will record the agreed return plan.
6 language and precision notes
Between and
Use both endpoints to communicate a real time window.
Until
Until eleven identifies the call's expected duration, not an entry authorization.
Now versus later
Declining now does not necessarily reject all future service.
Can return
State an available option without silently converting it to an exact appointment.
At the door
This specifies a limited handover location, not access to the whole room.
Expected
An expected end time remains different from a completed event.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which read-back is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Return between 11:15 and 11:30; no towels requested now.

The read-back preserves the chosen window and declined towel option.

2. What does the call ending at eleven establish?

Answer and explanation

C. Expected availability information, not automatic entry permission

An expected end time does not replace the actual room-access procedure.

3. Which offer is within Lina's stated options?

Answer and explanation

C. Fresh towels at the door now

Doorstep towels are one of the two explicitly available choices.

4. Why record the exact window?

Answer and explanation

D. To avoid turning a time range into a different appointment

Recording both endpoints prevents a later worker from assuming an unsupported exact arrival time.

E Conversations

Not during the call

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Alex: Guest in room 312 / Lina: Room attendant

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • entry procedure
  • service deferral
  • fresh towels
  • guest preference
  • confirmation
  • availability
  • return window
  • visit plan
  • declined service
  • time range
  1. Alex
    Sorry, this is not a good time to clean. I have a call until about eleven.
  2. Lina
    Of course. I will mark this visit as 1 for now. Would you like me to come back later?
  3. Alex
    Yes, please. Could you come at eleven, as soon as the call finishes?
  4. Lina
    My available 2 is eleven fifteen to eleven thirty. Would that work?
  5. Alex
    It should. Is there another option if the call goes on longer?
  6. Lina
    I can offer 3 at the door now, if that is all you need today.
  7. Alex
    No, I would rather have the room cleaned later. I do not need towels brought now.
  8. Lina
    Understood. I will record that 4 so another attendant does not bring them during your call.
  9. Alex
    Thanks. Did you say eleven fifteen or eleven fifty? I did not quite catch it.
  10. Lina
    Eleven fifteen to eleven thirty. It is a 5, rather than an exact arrival time.
  11. Alex
    Got it, fifteen to thirty. What happens if I am still on the call when you return?
  12. Lina
    We will follow our 6. We will not assume the room can be entered just because we arranged a return.
  13. Alex
    Good. I expect to finish at eleven, but the person I am speaking with sometimes runs late.
  14. Lina
    Thanks for explaining your 7. I will keep that separate from the later visit time.
  15. Alex
    I do still want cleaning today. Please do not cancel it because I said no just now.
  16. Lina
    I have it as a 8, not a cancellation for the day.
  17. Alex
    And this is for three twelve? I want to make sure I am not giving you the wrong room number.
  18. Lina
    Yes, room three hundred twelve. The 9 is eleven fifteen to eleven thirty, with no towels requested now.
  19. Alex
    That is right. Thanks for working around the call.
  20. Lina
    Thank you for the 10. I will let you get back to it and record the return window.

Answers and explanations
  1. declined service Turn 2. Declined service refers to the current visit, not a cancellation of all later cleaning.
  2. return window Turn 4. The return window is 11:15-11:30, not an exact 11:00 appointment.
  3. fresh towels Turn 6. Fresh towels are the separate doorstep option available now, not a request the guest has already accepted.
  4. guest preference Turn 8. Guest preference is the later cleaning visit, without a separate towel delivery now.
  5. time range Turn 10. A time range retains both endpoints instead of turning the plan into a single promised arrival time.
  6. entry procedure Turn 12. The entry procedure still governs the later visit; arranging a time does not remove the access requirements.
  7. availability Turn 14. Availability describes when the guest expects to be free, not a guarantee or automatic permission to enter.
  8. service deferral Turn 16. Service deferral means postponement, matching the chosen later visit rather than an all-day cancellation.
  9. visit plan Turn 18. Visit plan connects room 312 to the 11:15-11:30 return and the declined towel offer.
  10. confirmation Turn 20. Confirmation establishes agreement on the plan, not completed cleaning or unrestricted room access.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: Sorry, this is not a good time to clean. I have a call until about eleven.
  2. Lina: Of course. I will mark this visit as declined service for now. Would you like me to come back later?
  3. Alex: Yes, please. Could you come at eleven, as soon as the call finishes?
  4. Lina: My available return window is eleven fifteen to eleven thirty. Would that work?
  5. Alex: It should. Is there another option if the call goes on longer?
  6. Lina: I can offer fresh towels at the door now, if that is all you need today.
  7. Alex: No, I would rather have the room cleaned later. I do not need towels brought now.
  8. Lina: Understood. I will record that guest preference so another attendant does not bring them during your call.
  9. Alex: Thanks. Did you say eleven fifteen or eleven fifty? I did not quite catch it.
  10. Lina: Eleven fifteen to eleven thirty. It is a time range, rather than an exact arrival time.
  11. Alex: Got it, fifteen to thirty. What happens if I am still on the call when you return?
  12. Lina: We will follow our entry procedure. We will not assume the room can be entered just because we arranged a return.
  13. Alex: Good. I expect to finish at eleven, but the person I am speaking with sometimes runs late.
  14. Lina: Thanks for explaining your availability. I will keep that separate from the later visit time.
  15. Alex: I do still want cleaning today. Please do not cancel it because I said no just now.
  16. Lina: I have it as a service deferral, not a cancellation for the day.
  17. Alex: And this is for three twelve? I want to make sure I am not giving you the wrong room number.
  18. Lina: Yes, room three hundred twelve. The visit plan is eleven fifteen to eleven thirty, with no towels requested now.
  19. Alex: That is right. Thanks for working around the call.
  20. Lina: Thank you for the confirmation. I will let you get back to it and record the return window.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A service choice at the door

Room 312 at 10:00, following the case.

  1. Attendant: Good morning. Is now a convenient time to clean your room, or should I return?
  2. Guest: I'm on a work call until eleven, so I can't have the room cleaned now.
  3. Attendant: I can return between eleven fifteen and eleven thirty, or bring towels to the door now.
  4. Guest: Towels only would be better today. I don't need a later cleaning visit.
  5. Attendant: Certainly. How many fresh towels would you like delivered without entering the room?
  6. Guest: Two bath towels, please. I can take them at the door once you return.
  7. Attendant: I'll bring two and record that you declined room cleaning today, not all service.
  8. Guest: Thank you. Please don't interpret my declining cleaning as permission to enter after the call.
  9. Attendant: Understood. I'll leave the room entry unchanged and knock when I bring the towels.
  10. Guest: That works. I can briefly answer the door for them without interrupting the whole call.
A meeting runs beyond the access window

A cleaner negotiates access with an office occupant.

  1. Cleaner: Our access window starts at six, but I can see your meeting is still running.
  2. Office occupant: It may take another twenty minutes. Could you start cleaning around us while we finish?
  3. Cleaner: I need to check whether occupied cleaning is suitable under this site's arrangement.
  4. Office occupant: Then could you work on the corridor first and return after we leave?
  5. Cleaner: The corridor is on my assignment, so I can change that order of work.
  6. Office occupant: Thank you. We expect to leave by six twenty, but I can't guarantee that yet.
  7. Cleaner: I'll return to ask at six twenty rather than assume the room has become available.
  8. Office occupant: Please do. I'll tell the team someone will check, not announce cleaning as already started.
  9. Cleaner: If you're still here, I'll report the access delay and discuss the remaining window with my lead.
  10. Office occupant: That sounds reasonable. You can complete the corridor while we finish the meeting.
A sign and a verbal message conflict

An attendant asks the supervisor about contradictory room-access information.

  1. Attendant: Room 418 still has a do-not-disturb notice, but my sheet says service requested this morning.
  2. Supervisor: Do you have a current message from the guest resolving that difference, or only the sheet?
  3. Attendant: Only the sheet. I haven't entered or assumed the sign was left there accidentally.
  4. Supervisor: Thank you. We'll clarify the guest's current preference through the property's access arrangements.
  5. Attendant: Should I mark the room as cleaned because it appeared on the requested-service list?
  6. Supervisor: No. Mark the service as not completed due to unresolved access, using our status categories.
  7. Attendant: I'll state that the notice is present and the request on the sheet needs clarification.
  8. Supervisor: Exactly. Don't turn either piece of information into a guess about the guest's intentions.
  9. Attendant: I'll move to my next available room while you check the access question.
  10. Supervisor: Please do. I'll give you a clear update rather than leave you waiting at that door.

  2. Repeat the room number, both window endpoints, and the declined towel option.

Record another return window

Answer and explanation

B. 420

Room 420 is the identifier supplied for the new visit plan.

2. Guest: "The call is expected to finish at ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 10:00

Ten o'clock is the expected call end, not the promised return.

3. Attendant: "The return window is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 10:30-10:45

The offered and chosen window runs from ten thirty to ten forty-five.

4. Attendant: "Entry still follows the actual ___."

Answer and explanation

C. access procedure

Scheduling the visit does not remove the site's entry requirements.

Communication goal: Acknowledge a declined visit, offer the actual return window, and distinguish an agreed plan from room-entry permission.

Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Certainly; I will not start service now. / Your call is expected to finish at eleven. / I can return between eleven fifteen and eleven thirty. / Eleven is not available, but eleven fifteen to eleven thirty is. / I can offer fresh towels at the door now. / I can hand the towels to you at the door. / Which of those options would suit you? / I have recorded the eleven-fifteen-to-eleven-thirty window. / Cleaning was declined at ten because of your call. / You are postponing this visit, not cancelling every service. / I understand that you do not want an interruption. / We will still follow the entry procedure when we return. / No towel delivery is requested now. / This plan is for room 312. / Have I understood your preference correctly? / Thank you; I will record the agreed return plan.
Vocabulary: occupied room: Room currently in use by a guest or other occupant. / room attendant: Worker assigned to guest-room cleaning and related service. / declined service: Offered service the occupant does not accept at that time. / return window: Time range in which a worker can come back. / appointment time: Specific agreed time rather than a broad window. / privacy: Freedom from inappropriate intrusion or disclosure. / access permission: Authorization to enter or use a particular space. / entry procedure: Actual required steps and rules for entering a space. / doorstep delivery: Handover of an item at the doorway rather than room entry. / fresh towels: Clean towels available for guest use. / service option: Available choice about the service to be provided. / do-not-disturb notice: Guest instruction or signal requesting no interruption under site rules. / service request: Guest's stated request for an available service. / visit plan: Arrangement for a future visit. / availability: Time or circumstances in which a person or service can be accessed. / time range: Interval with a beginning and end. / scheduled return: Planned later visit within the agreed arrangement. / unoccupied: Not currently occupied, where that status is actually established. / guest preference: Stated service choice or wish of the guest. / access record: Record of relevant entry or permission information. / interruption: Action that disturbs an ongoing activity. / service deferral: Moving a service to a later available opportunity. / exact-time promise: Commitment to a specific arrival time. / confirmation: Clear agreement about the understood plan.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Clarifying product and task informationThe unreadable label+

Your goal

Report uncertain product identity, request understandable information, and resist identification by color or familiarity.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

The unreadable label

Cleaner Farah notices an unreadable label on a bottle on the cleaning cart. Colleague Owen believes it is the usual surface cleaner because of its color, but two products use similar bottles. Farah has not used it. Supervisor Ben can identify the product through the actual site process and provide the relevant information or a clearly identified replacement. No identity, mixing instruction, dilution ratio, protective-equipment selection, or use approval is established here. The task is to ask precise questions and preserve the stopped-use status while the uncertainty is resolved.

Farah: Cleaner / Owen: Colleague

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is wrong with the bottle?

Answer and explanation

A. Its label is unreadable

The visible problem is an unreadable label, leaving identity unconfirmed.

2. Why is color insufficient here?

Answer and explanation

B. Two products use similar bottles

Similar bottles make appearance an unreliable basis for identifying this product.

3. What can Ben arrange?

Answer and explanation

B. Proper identification and information, or an identified replacement

Ben is the named supervisor able to resolve identity through the actual site process.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A product identifier must connect the actual container with the relevant information. A similar bottle or familiar color does not establish identity. Do not infer use instructions, surface suitability, or protection requirements from an unverified guess.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
product identifier
Name or code used to identify a specific chemical product.verify the product identifier
unreadable label
Label whose information cannot be reliably read.report an unreadable label
container
Vessel holding a substance or product.identify the container
secondary container
Container into which a product has been transferred from another container.check a secondary container
safety data sheet
Document describing a chemical product's hazards and relevant safety information.consult the safety data sheet
SDS
Abbreviation for safety data sheet.obtain the relevant SDS
hazard statement
Standardized wording describing the nature of a chemical hazard.read the hazard statement
precautionary statement
Wording describing measures to prevent or reduce harmful effects.understand the precautionary statement
signal word
Label word indicating the relative severity of a hazard.identify the signal word
pictogram
Graphic symbol communicating a hazard or other defined information.recognize the pictogram
contact time
Required period for the specified product use; a disinfected surface must remain wet as its label directs.verify the wet contact time
manufacturer instructions
Directions supplied by the product or equipment manufacturer.follow manufacturer instructions
surface compatibility
Whether a product is suitable for a particular surface material.verify surface compatibility
cleaner
Product intended to remove soil or unwanted material from a surface.identify the cleaner
detergent
Cleaning agent that helps remove soil.identify the detergent
disinfectant
Product intended to kill or inactivate specified microorganisms when used as directed.identify the disinfectant
sanitizer
Product used to reduce microorganisms to an applicable specified level.distinguish a sanitizer
dilution
Reduction in concentration according to the applicable instructions.verify dilution instructions
concentrate
Product supplied at a strength requiring the specified preparation before use.identify a concentrate
ready-to-use
Supplied in a form intended for use without additional dilution, as directed.confirm ready-to-use status
PPE
Personal protective equipment selected for the actual hazards and task.verify required PPE
ventilation
Supply or movement of air relevant to working conditions.check ventilation requirements
exposure
Contact with a substance through a relevant route.report an exposure
identified replacement
Substitute product with its identity and relevant information established.request an identified replacement

Cleaning, sanitizing, and disinfecting are different functions. The required product and process depend on the actual task and site. This vocabulary does not authorize a chemical procedure or replace understandable training and current product instructions.

C Notice the language

Question the evidence, not the colleague

A familiar coworker may offer a confident shortcut in good faith. Explain the specific uncertainty, such as two similar bottles, and request a reliable identification. This keeps the discussion cooperative while making clear that confidence or seniority cannot replace the missing product information.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the problem
I cannot read this bottle label.
Preserve the status
I have not used the product.
Question the identification
What reliable information identifies this container?
Explain the ambiguity
Two products use similar bottles here.
Reject a color-only guess
The color does not establish which product this is.
Ask for the supervisor
Can Ben verify the identity through the site process?
Request information
I need the relevant product information in a form I can understand.
Ask for the document
Which safety data sheet belongs to this product?
Check the match
The information must match the actual container.
Request a replacement
A clearly identified replacement may resolve the immediate problem.
Avoid a guessed ratio
I will not invent dilution instructions.
Separate compatibility
We also need the product to suit the assigned surface.
Keep the pause visible
The product remains unused while identity is unresolved.
Ask for clarification
Please explain that instruction before I proceed.
Avoid a false approval
We have not confirmed this product for the task.
Close the request
Let us get verified identification and the relevant instructions.
6 language and precision notes
Looks like
This reports resemblance, not verified identity.
The relevant
The relevant SDS must match the actual product rather than a similar bottle.
Have not used
This clearly states the current status without implying exposure.
Before proceeding
This establishes the sequence of clarification before action.
Understandable
Receiving a document is not the same as understanding the instruction.
Replacement
A replacement needs clear identification; another unknown bottle does not solve the problem.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response challenges the shortcut constructively?

Answer and explanation

B. Two products look similar; let us ask Ben to verify this one.

The response gives the factual reason for doubt and a proper clarification route.

2. Which document is relevant?

Answer and explanation

B. The safety data sheet matching the actual product

Safety information needs to correspond to the identified product rather than an approximate match.

3. Which statement is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. This product is approved for the surface because the bottle looks familiar.

Familiar appearance does not establish product identity or suitability for the task.

4. What should remain clear in the handoff?

Answer and explanation

D. Identity unresolved and product unused

The handoff must preserve the actual uncertainty and stopped-use status supplied.

Answers and explanations
  1. looks like Turn 2. Looks like expresses visual resemblance, which is weaker than verified identity when two products use similar bottles.
  2. product identifier Turn 4. Product identifier is the reliable name or code needed to connect the container with the correct product information.
  3. unreadable label Turn 6. Unreadable label is the observed problem that prevents reliable identification, rather than an assumed chemical fault.
  4. product information Turn 8. Product information must relate to the identified product and task; a name alone does not explain all relevant requirements.
  5. safety data sheet Turn 10. Safety data sheet expands SDS and identifies the document describing the product's hazards and related safety information.
  6. identified replacement Turn 12. An identified replacement offers a product with established identity and information, not a second uncertain container.
  7. Surface compatibility Turn 14. Surface compatibility concerns whether the identified product suits the actual material; products are not interchangeable merely because both clean.
  8. dilution Turn 16. Dilution instructions are product-specific requirements that must be verified rather than invented from past experience.
  9. unused Turn 18. Unused states the actual status of this bottle in Farah's task; the message must not invent use or exposure.
  10. clarification Turn 20. Clarification resolves a specific uncertainty before action; receiving unclear information does not itself establish understanding or permission.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Farah: I cannot read this bottle label. I have not used it. Do we have a reliable way to identify what is inside?
  2. Owen: It looks like the surface cleaner we usually use. The color is familiar, although I have not checked a readable name or code on this one.
  3. Farah: Two products use bottles like this. I do not want to choose from the color alone.
  4. Owen: You are right to separate appearance from the product identifier. I cannot give you a verified identity just from recognizing the general bottle style.
  5. Farah: Can Ben help identify it? I would like to give him a precise question instead of asking whether the bottle is probably all right.
  6. Owen: Yes. We can report the unreadable label and say the product remains unused. He can arrange identification through the actual site process.
  7. Farah: If he identifies it, I also need the relevant instructions. Knowing a name would not tell me every requirement for this particular surface.
  8. Owen: We should request the matching product information, including the appropriate label and safety information. We should not fill in missing directions from memory.
  9. Farah: Could you remind me what SDS stands for? I want to ask for the document that actually matches the product.
  10. Owen: SDS means safety data sheet. The important point is that it must match the actual product, not merely another container that looks similar.
  11. Farah: Thank you. And if the bottle cannot be identified promptly, could we ask for a different one with clear information instead?
  12. Owen: Ben can arrange an identified replacement through the site process. That means a product with clear identification and relevant instructions, not another unknown bottle.
  13. Farah: If Ben gives us a replacement, I still need to check that it is right for the surface, correct?
  14. Owen: Agreed. Surface compatibility still matters. We need the appropriate product for the assigned task, with the actual instructions and training requirements followed.
  15. Farah: And any dilution has to come from those instructions, not the ratio I used at my last job.
  16. Owen: Correct. Any dilution instruction must come from the applicable information. This conversation has not established a ratio or authorized any preparation step.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The blue bottle isn't identified

The unreadable-label case at a cleaning cart.

  1. Cleaner: This bottle's label is unreadable, and two products on the cart look similar.
  2. Supervisor: Have you used the bottle, or are you asking before starting that task?
  3. Cleaner: I haven't used it. A colleague recognized the color, but couldn't confirm the contents.
  4. Supervisor: Thanks for stopping to ask. I'll arrange identification or a clearly labeled replacement.
  5. Cleaner: Could you also show me the current product label and Safety Data Sheet for the replacement?
  6. Supervisor: Yes. We'll use information matching the actual product, not a sheet for something similar.
  7. Cleaner: The room task is waiting. Should I describe the delay as a product-information problem?
  8. Supervisor: That's accurate. Don't say the product is defective; we haven't established that.
  9. Cleaner: I'll complete another assigned task that doesn't depend on this bottle while you check.
  10. Supervisor: Good. I'll tell you when the product-information issue is resolved and the task can be reassigned or resumed.
Cleaning isn't a disinfection claim

A cleaner clarifies a client's description of completed work.

  1. Client: The tables look clean. Can I tell everyone they have definitely been disinfected?
  2. Cleaner: I can confirm the cleaning task I completed, but appearance doesn't establish a disinfection claim.
  3. Client: Our meeting notice uses both words together. I assumed they meant exactly the same thing.
  4. Cleaner: They describe different tasks. My supervisor can confirm the agreed standard for this room.
  5. Client: Please ask them which work was actually included, rather than change the wording by guesswork.
  6. Cleaner: I will. The record shows table cleaning completed; it doesn't document the additional claim you're asking about.
  7. Client: Then I'll say the tables were cleaned while the other question is checked.
  8. Cleaner: That accurately describes what I can confirm from my assignment and completion record.
  9. Client: Could you have the supervisor speak to me if the agreed task needs changing?
  10. Cleaner: Yes. I'll pass on your question about the service standard, not promise a new treatment myself.
A different label on a familiar product

A cleaner notices a revised product name before starting work.

  1. Cleaner: The replacement container has a different product name from the one on our task sheet.
  2. Team lead: Is it a confirmed replacement on today's list, or just a similar-looking container?
  3. Cleaner: I don't know. The delivery person said it was the new version, but I haven't verified that.
  4. Team lead: Let's check the actual product information and whether the task sheet needs updating.
  5. Cleaner: I'll leave that task pending rather than copy instructions from the previous product.
  6. Team lead: Please identify the old and new names so the supervisor can review the difference.
  7. Cleaner: I've got both names here. The container size is the same, but that doesn't establish equivalence.
  8. Team lead: Correct. We need a confirmed match to the assigned work, not a comparison of bottle size.
  9. Cleaner: I'll ask for the correct label and Safety Data Sheet before using the replacement.
  10. Team lead: I'll arrange that check and let the client know this task hasn't been completed yet.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The unreadable label
  1. Read the label exchange, distinguishing appearance from a verified identifier.
  2. Say what SDS stands for, then repeat the request for matching product information.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report another unidentified container

A spray bottle on cart C has an unreadable label. Worker Ana has not used it. Lead Leon can arrange identification or an identified replacement. Product color is not sufficient evidence.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "The affected bottle is on cart ___."

Answer and explanation

A. C

Cart C is the stated location of the unidentified bottle.

2. Worker: "The label is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unreadable

Unreadable describes the observed label problem preventing reliable identification.

3. Colleague: "The lead for this query is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Leon

Leon is the named lead who can arrange the check.

4. Worker: "The product remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unused

Ana has not used the bottle, so that status must remain clear.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
