Lesson 03: Clarifying product and task information
Original fictional case: Cleaner Farah notices an unreadable label on a bottle on the cleaning cart. Colleague Owen believes it is the usual surface cleaner because of its color, but two products use similar bottles. Farah has not used it. Supervisor Ben can identify the product through the actual site process and provide the relevant information or a clearly identified replacement. No identity, mixing instruction, dilution ratio, protective-equipment selection, or use approval is established here. The task is to ask precise questions and preserve the stopped-use status while the uncertainty is resolved.
Language workshop: The unreadable label
Communication goal: Report uncertain product identity, request understandable information, and resist identification by color or familiarity.
Language guidance: A product identifier must connect the actual container with the relevant information. A similar bottle or familiar color does not establish identity. Do not infer use instructions, surface suitability, or protection requirements from an unverified guess.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I cannot read this bottle label. / I have not used the product. / What reliable information identifies this container? / Two products use similar bottles here. / The color does not establish which product this is. / Can Ben verify the identity through the site process? / I need the relevant product information in a form I can understand. / Which safety data sheet belongs to this product? / The information must match the actual container. / A clearly identified replacement may resolve the immediate problem. / I will not invent dilution instructions. / We also need the product to suit the assigned surface. / The product remains unused while identity is unresolved. / Please explain that instruction before I proceed. / We have not confirmed this product for the task. / Let us get verified identification and the relevant instructions.
Vocabulary: product identifier: Name or code used to identify a specific chemical product. / unreadable label: Label whose information cannot be reliably read. / container: Vessel holding a substance or product. / secondary container: Container into which a product has been transferred from another container. / safety data sheet: Document describing a chemical product's hazards and relevant safety information. / SDS: Abbreviation for safety data sheet. / hazard statement: Standardized wording describing the nature of a chemical hazard. / precautionary statement: Wording describing measures to prevent or reduce harmful effects. / signal word: Label word indicating the relative severity of a hazard. / pictogram: Graphic symbol communicating a hazard or other defined information. / contact time: Required period for the specified product use; a disinfected surface must remain wet as its label directs. / manufacturer instructions: Directions supplied by the product or equipment manufacturer. / surface compatibility: Whether a product is suitable for a particular surface material. / cleaner: Product intended to remove soil or unwanted material from a surface. / detergent: Cleaning agent that helps remove soil. / disinfectant: Product intended to kill or inactivate specified microorganisms when used as directed. / sanitizer: Product used to reduce microorganisms to an applicable specified level. / dilution: Reduction in concentration according to the applicable instructions. / concentrate: Product supplied at a strength requiring the specified preparation before use. / ready-to-use: Supplied in a form intended for use without additional dilution, as directed. / PPE: Personal protective equipment selected for the actual hazards and task. / ventilation: Supply or movement of air relevant to working conditions. / exposure: Contact with a substance through a relevant route. / identified replacement: Substitute product with its identity and relevant information established.
Speaking task: Read the label exchange, distinguishing appearance from a verified identifier. Say what SDS stands for, then repeat the request for matching product information. Complete the cart C report without adding an identity, dilution, or use approval.
Partner: Farah: Cleaner / Owen: Colleague
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A spray bottle on cart C has an unreadable label. Worker Ana has not used it. Lead Leon can arrange identification or an identified replacement. Product color is not sufficient evidence.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Farah: Please tell Ben that I have not used this bottle. That is important for the report.
Owen: I will preserve that unused status in the message. We should also keep any exposure or incident report factual if something different ever occurs.
Farah: Good. Once the information is available, I will ask about any part I do not understand before I proceed with the assigned task.
Owen: That is the point of clarification. Let us ask Ben for reliable identification and understandable instructions, keeping the product unused while this uncertainty remains.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Reporting faults and unsafe accessOutside washroom B+

Your goal

Give an exact location and observed condition, preserve uncertainty about the cause, and avoid premature clearance.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Outside washroom B

Cleaner Ravi sees water on the floor outside washroom B, beside the east stair door. Ravi does not know its source and has already reported it under the site safety arrangements. Supervisor Elena asks for a clear follow-up message identifying the location and current status. The affected area has not been declared ready. No leak diagnosis, repair result, completed cleanup, or approved reopening is established. The exchange practices accurate reporting while the actual site controls remain in effect; it does not teach a cleanup method or authorize anyone to enter the affected area.

Ravi: Cleaner / Elena: Supervisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Where is the water?

Answer and explanation

D. Outside washroom B, beside the east stair door

The brief supplies both the washroom identifier and the east-door landmark.

2. What does Ravi know about the source?

Answer and explanation

A. It is unknown

Ravi has observed water but has not established its source.

3. What is the readiness status?

Answer and explanation

B. The affected area has not been declared ready

Reporting the condition does not establish completed controls or clearance.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Outside washroom B and beside the east stair door locate the water. They do not establish that it came from the washroom or stairs. Keep the observed location separate from the unknown source.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
observed condition
What the worker directly notices at the location.report the observed condition
exact location
Specific place described with enough detail to identify it.give the exact location
landmark
Recognizable feature used to locate another place.name a clear landmark
washroom
Room containing toilet and washing facilities.identify the washroom
stair door
Door providing access to a stairway.identify the stair door
corridor
Passage connecting rooms or areas.locate the corridor
threshold
Floor-level boundary at a doorway.identify the threshold
stair landing
Level platform between flights or at a stair entrance.describe the stair landing
visible water
Water that can be directly seen.report visible water
source
Origin of the observed condition.investigate the source
leak
Unintended escape of liquid from a system or container.verify a suspected leak
slip hazard
Condition that may cause a person to slip.report a slip hazard
trip hazard
Obstacle or condition that may cause a person to trip.distinguish a trip hazard
affected area
Specific area involved in an incident or condition.identify the affected area
site controls
Actual measures governing safety and access at a location.follow site controls
hazard report
Message recording a potentially harmful condition.submit a hazard report
maintenance referral
Transfer of a fault concern to the relevant maintenance team.arrange a maintenance referral
fault diagnosis
Determination of the cause or nature of a defect.avoid an unsupported fault diagnosis
status update
Report of the current state of the matter.provide a status update
clearance
Required authorization that an area or item may return to the specified use.await clearance
reopening
Making an area available again after the relevant controls and approval.confirm reopening
readiness
State of being prepared and authorized for the specified next use.verify readiness
report acknowledgement
Confirmation that the hazard message was received.obtain report acknowledgement
follow-up owner
Person responsible for the next check or update.name the follow-up owner

Reported, assessed, cleaned, repaired, and cleared describe different stages. A received report cannot serve as reopening approval. Actual hazard controls and emergency arrangements take precedence over this language exercise.

C Notice the language

Urgency needs precision

A brief hazard message can be both direct and respectful. Lead with the location and observed condition, then state what is unknown and who has been informed. Do not soften a hazard into nothing serious or strengthen a location clue into an unsupported diagnosis.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with location
Water is visible outside washroom B.
Add the landmark
It is beside the east stair door.
State uncertainty
I do not know the source.
Avoid a diagnosis
I am not confirming a plumbing fault.
Confirm reporting
I have reported it under the site arrangements.
Preserve the status
The area has not been declared ready.
Ask for acknowledgement
Please confirm you have the correct location.
Correct direction
East stair door, not west.
Keep observations factual
I am reporting what I can see.
Avoid minimizing
I cannot describe it as harmless.
Keep controls active
The actual site controls still apply.
Request the next owner
Who is taking the follow-up?
Separate report and action
Receipt of the report does not mean the work is complete.
Avoid reopening language
I have no clearance to report.
Give a bounded update
The location is confirmed; the source remains unknown.
Close the message
Please keep the current status attached to that location.
6 language and precision notes
Outside versus inside
This contrast can direct a responder to the correct side of a doorway.
Beside
A landmark locates a condition without identifying its cause.
Visible
This describes direct observation, not a diagnosis or measurement.
Has not been declared
The phrase preserves the absence of authorized readiness.
Still
Use still to carry an unresolved status into the next message.
East versus west
Repeat direction explicitly when a similar landmark exists elsewhere.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which message is most precise?

Answer and explanation

B. Water outside washroom B, beside the east stair door; source unknown.

The message supplies the exact location and preserves uncertainty about the source.

2. Which inference is not supported?

Answer and explanation

D. The water definitely comes from a broken pipe.

An observed wet floor does not establish a particular plumbing cause.

3. What does report acknowledgement mean?

Answer and explanation

C. The message was received, not that the area is cleared

Acknowledgement confirms receipt only and must not be confused with operational clearance.

4. What should a follow-up preserve?

Answer and explanation

D. Location, observed condition, unknown source, and current readiness status

These four details maintain an accurate and usable report without unsupported conclusions.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

Outside washroom B

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ravi: Cleaner / Elena: Supervisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • east stair door
  • clearance
  • status update
  • fault diagnosis
  • observed condition
  • source
  • report acknowledgement
  • outside washroom B
  • hazard report
  • affected area
  1. Elena
    Following up on your earlier hazard report: where exactly is the water, and what is the current status?
  2. Ravi
    It is 1, beside the east stair door. Water is visible on the floor there; I do not know where it came from.
  3. Elena
    East stair door, not west? There are two similar entrances.
  4. Ravi
    Yes, the 2. I am using it as the landmark for the location, not claiming that the water came from the stairway.
  5. Elena
    Do we know the source, or have you only seen water on the floor?
  6. Ravi
    The 3 remains unknown to me. I have not diagnosed a pipe, a drain, or any other cause from the location alone.
  7. Elena
    Have you already reported the condition through the site arrangements? I do not want this follow-up conversation to replace the required safety response.
  8. Ravi
    Yes, I have made the 4 under those arrangements. The actual site controls still apply while the condition is being addressed.
  9. Elena
    Good. Please also state whether the affected area has been declared ready. I need to avoid passing on an accidental all-clear.
  10. Ravi
    There is no 5 for me to report. The area has not been declared ready, and my report should not be read as approval to use it.
  11. Elena
    I will retain that status. Give me one short observation to pass to the response team.
  12. Ravi
    The 6 is water on the floor outside washroom B, beside the east stair door. That is what I can state directly.
  13. Elena
    Some people may hear near the washroom and assume a plumbing leak. I will make clear that the source still needs appropriate assessment.
  14. Ravi
    Thank you. A 7 has not been made by me. The location helps someone find the condition, but it does not prove what caused it.
  15. Elena
    I have the report. I will not mark cleanup or repair complete from this call.
  16. Ravi
    Exactly. Your 8 tells me the message arrived. It should not be turned into a completed-work entry or a statement that the area is ready.
  17. Elena
    I will coordinate the follow-up through the site process and keep the responsible people informed. No reopening decision is being made in this exchange.
  18. Ravi
    Then I will keep the 9 factual: location confirmed, source unknown, area not declared ready. I will report any new observation through the same arrangements.
  19. Elena
    Please keep outside and east in the next handover. Those details will help the team find the right place.
  20. Ravi
    I will. The 10 remains outside washroom B beside the east stair door. I will not replace that precise report with a guessed cause or an all-clear.

Answers and explanations
  1. outside washroom B Turn 2. Outside washroom B identifies the specific side and room, narrowing the location beyond a general washroom area.
  2. east stair door Turn 4. East stair door is the supplied landmark and direction, preventing confusion with another stair entrance.
  3. source Turn 6. Source means the origin of the water, which has not been determined from the visible condition.
  4. hazard report Turn 8. Hazard report is the communication already made under site arrangements; it does not establish completed response or clearance.
  5. clearance Turn 10. Clearance is the required authorization to return an area to the specified use; none is established here.
  6. observed condition Turn 12. Observed condition is the visible water at the named location, not a theory about how it arrived.
  7. fault diagnosis Turn 14. Fault diagnosis requires a determination beyond this observation; the cleaner has not established a plumbing failure.
  8. report acknowledgement Turn 16. Report acknowledgement confirms receipt of the information only, not completion of cleanup, repair, or inspection.
  9. status update Turn 18. Status update must preserve the current unresolved condition and absence of readiness rather than imply progress not established.
  10. affected area Turn 20. Affected area identifies the particular location requiring continued attention; it must not be replaced by a vague building-wide statement.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: Following up on your earlier hazard report: where exactly is the water, and what is the current status?
  2. Ravi: It is outside washroom B, beside the east stair door. Water is visible on the floor there; I do not know where it came from.
  3. Elena: East stair door, not west? There are two similar entrances.
  4. Ravi: Yes, the east stair door. I am using it as the landmark for the location, not claiming that the water came from the stairway.
  5. Elena: Do we know the source, or have you only seen water on the floor?
  6. Ravi: The source remains unknown to me. I have not diagnosed a pipe, a drain, or any other cause from the location alone.
  7. Elena: Have you already reported the condition through the site arrangements? I do not want this follow-up conversation to replace the required safety response.
  8. Ravi: Yes, I have made the hazard report under those arrangements. The actual site controls still apply while the condition is being addressed.
  9. Elena: Good. Please also state whether the affected area has been declared ready. I need to avoid passing on an accidental all-clear.
  10. Ravi: There is no clearance for me to report. The area has not been declared ready, and my report should not be read as approval to use it.
  11. Elena: I will retain that status. Give me one short observation to pass to the response team.
  12. Ravi: The observed condition is water on the floor outside washroom B, beside the east stair door. That is what I can state directly.
  13. Elena: Some people may hear near the washroom and assume a plumbing leak. I will make clear that the source still needs appropriate assessment.
  14. Ravi: Thank you. A fault diagnosis has not been made by me. The location helps someone find the condition, but it does not prove what caused it.
  15. Elena: I have the report. I will not mark cleanup or repair complete from this call.
  16. Ravi: Exactly. Your report acknowledgement tells me the message arrived. It should not be turned into a completed-work entry or a statement that the area is ready.
  17. Elena: I will coordinate the follow-up through the site process and keep the responsible people informed. No reopening decision is being made in this exchange.
  18. Ravi: Then I will keep the status update factual: location confirmed, source unknown, area not declared ready. I will report any new observation through the same arrangements.
  19. Elena: Please keep outside and east in the next handover. Those details will help the team find the right place.
  20. Ravi: I will. The affected area remains outside washroom B beside the east stair door. I will not replace that precise report with a guessed cause or an all-clear.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Water beside the east stair door

The washroom B report in the case.

  1. Cleaner: There's water outside washroom B, beside the east stair door on the ground floor.
  2. Supervisor: Do you know the source, or have you only observed water in that location?
  3. Cleaner: Only the water. I haven't identified a leak or marked the affected area ready.
  4. Supervisor: I'll arrange the site's response. Have you reported the access concern through our usual contact?
  5. Cleaner: Yes, the area concern has been reported, but I need to know who takes the follow-up.
  6. Supervisor: I'll take that contact and tell the response team the exact washroom and stair-door location.
  7. Cleaner: A passerby said the floor was cleaned earlier. That doesn't explain the water now.
  8. Supervisor: Agreed. We'll use the current observation, not assume the earlier cleaning settled today's condition.
  9. Cleaner: What should the hazard report say for the incoming team?
  10. Supervisor: Tell them water was reported, the source is unknown, and I'm coordinating the response.
The noise is not a diagnosis

A cleaner reports a vacuum problem without repair instructions.

  1. Cleaner: The vacuum labeled C4 made an unfamiliar rattling sound while I was working near reception.
  2. Team lead: Have you stopped using it, and do you know whether anything specific caused the sound?
  3. Cleaner: I've stopped using it. I don't know the cause and haven't attempted a repair.
  4. Team lead: I'll arrange a check and an out-of-service notice for C4, not C14.
  5. Cleaner: The reception carpet task is incomplete. Could you confirm whether another suitable unit is available?
  6. Team lead: I'll check that. Don't report the task complete simply because most of the area was covered.
  7. Cleaner: Understood. The strip beside the visitor chairs remains unfinished, while the open aisle is complete.
  8. Team lead: That distinction helps. I'll assign the remaining strip once an equipment option is confirmed.
  9. Cleaner: Should I tell reception we need a motor replacement, as someone suggested?
  10. Team lead: No. Report the equipment check and incomplete strip; a motor diagnosis hasn't been established.
A recurring mark may need maintenance

A cleaner reports a recurring ceiling stain without identifying its cause.

  1. Cleaner: The mark above office 5's doorway is visible again after the earlier visit.
  2. Building contact: Are you saying there's a confirmed leak, or just that the mark has returned?
  3. Cleaner: Just that it has returned. I haven't established the source or a repair requirement.
  4. Building contact: I'll pass that observation to maintenance. When did you notice the change today?
  5. Cleaner: At about seven this morning, while completing the corridor assignment outside office five.
  6. Building contact: Is the office itself affected, or did you only observe the corridor-facing area?
  7. Cleaner: I observed the area above the doorway from the corridor. I haven't inspected inside.
  8. Building contact: Then I'll keep the request limited to that location and ask maintenance to assess it.
  9. Cleaner: Please tell me who receives the maintenance request so I can hand over the status accurately.
  10. Building contact: I've accepted it for the building team. Tell the next cleaner assessment is pending with me.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Outside washroom B
  1. Read the hazard follow-up, emphasizing outside and east.
  2. Repeat Ravi's exact location, observation, unknown source, and uncleared status.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report a different wet-floor location

Water is visible outside washroom D beside the west service door. The source is unknown. A report has been made, but the area has not been declared ready.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Cleaner: "The water is outside washroom ___."

Answer and explanation

A. D

Washroom D is the exact room identifier in the new report.

2. Supervisor: "The landmark is the ___ service door."

Answer and explanation

C. west

West identifies the correct service door rather than another direction.

3. Cleaner: "The source remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unknown

The visible condition does not establish where the water originated.

4. Supervisor: "The area is not declared ___."

Answer and explanation

B. ready

Reporting the condition does not provide a readiness declaration or clearance.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Lesson 04: Reporting faults and unsafe access
Original fictional case: Cleaner Ravi sees water on the floor outside washroom B, beside the east stair door. Ravi does not know its source and has already reported it under the site safety arrangements. Supervisor Elena asks for a clear follow-up message identifying the location and current status. The affected area has not been declared ready. No leak diagnosis, repair result, completed cleanup, or approved reopening is established. The exchange practices accurate reporting while the actual site controls remain in effect; it does not teach a cleanup method or authorize anyone to enter the affected area.
Language workshop: Outside washroom B
Communication goal: Give an exact location and observed condition, preserve uncertainty about the cause, and avoid premature clearance.
Language guidance: Outside washroom B and beside the east stair door locate the water. They do not establish that it came from the washroom or stairs. Keep the observed location separate from the unknown source.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Water is visible outside washroom B. / It is beside the east stair door. / I do not know the source. / I am not confirming a plumbing fault. / I have reported it under the site arrangements. / The area has not been declared ready. / Please confirm you have the correct location. / East stair door, not west. / I am reporting what I can see. / I cannot describe it as harmless. / The actual site controls still apply. / Who is taking the follow-up? / Receipt of the report does not mean the work is complete. / I have no clearance to report. / The location is confirmed; the source remains unknown. / Please keep the current status attached to that location.
Vocabulary: observed condition: What the worker directly notices at the location. / exact location: Specific place described with enough detail to identify it. / landmark: Recognizable feature used to locate another place. / washroom: Room containing toilet and washing facilities. / stair door: Door providing access to a stairway. / corridor: Passage connecting rooms or areas. / threshold: Floor-level boundary at a doorway. / stair landing: Level platform between flights or at a stair entrance. / visible water: Water that can be directly seen. / source: Origin of the observed condition. / leak: Unintended escape of liquid from a system or container. / slip hazard: Condition that may cause a person to slip. / trip hazard: Obstacle or condition that may cause a person to trip. / affected area: Specific area involved in an incident or condition. / site controls: Actual measures governing safety and access at a location. / hazard report: Message recording a potentially harmful condition. / maintenance referral: Transfer of a fault concern to the relevant maintenance team. / fault diagnosis: Determination of the cause or nature of a defect. / status update: Report of the current state of the matter. / clearance: Required authorization that an area or item may return to the specified use. / reopening: Making an area available again after the relevant controls and approval. / readiness: State of being prepared and authorized for the specified next use. / report acknowledgement: Confirmation that the hazard message was received. / follow-up owner: Person responsible for the next check or update.
Speaking task: Read the hazard follow-up, emphasizing outside and east. Repeat Ravi's exact location, observation, unknown source, and uncleared status. Complete the new west-door report without changing the location into a cause.
Partner: Ravi: Cleaner / Elena: Supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Water is visible outside washroom D beside the west service door. The source is unknown. A report has been made, but the area has not been declared ready.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Elena: I will coordinate the follow-up through the site process and keep the responsible people informed. No reopening decision is being made in this exchange.
Ravi: Then I will keep the status update factual: location confirmed, source unknown, area not declared ready. I will report any new observation through the same arrangements.
Elena: Please keep outside and east in the next handover. Those details will help the team find the right place.
Ravi: I will. The affected area remains outside washroom B beside the east stair door. I will not replace that precise report with a guessed cause or an all-clear.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Handling found items and privacy questionsThe closed black case+

Your goal

Describe found property without inspecting private contents, record its location, and confirm transfer to the designated receiver.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The closed black case

After checkout, attendant Ana finds a closed black glasses case beneath the chair in room 214. The owner is unknown, and Ana does not open the case. Dev is the designated lost-property receiver and can accept it through the site procedure. The handover should record only established details and confirm receipt. A room number does not by itself identify the owner, and the appearance of a glasses case does not confirm what is inside. Any later ownership claim or release requires the actual verification process, not a guess during this conversation.

Ana: Room attendant / Dev: Designated lost-property receiver

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Where was the case found?

Answer and explanation

C. Beneath the chair in room 214

Both the room number and the position beneath the chair are supplied.

2. What is known about the owner?

Answer and explanation

A. The owner is unknown

No ownership verification is supplied, so the room location cannot identify the owner.

3. Who is the designated receiver?

Answer and explanation

B. Dev

Dev is specifically identified as the receiver under the site found-property process.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Found after checkout is a timing fact, not proof that the last guest owns the item. Record the room and location accurately while leaving ownership unknown until the actual process verifies it.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
found property
Item discovered whose owner or custody needs appropriate handling.report found property
lost-property receiver
Designated person who accepts found items under the site process.contact the lost-property receiver
visible description
Account limited to details observable without inspecting hidden contents.give a visible description
glasses case
Container designed for spectacles, regardless of its actual contents.describe the glasses case
closed item
Item whose interior is not exposed or inspected.preserve the closed item
contents
Items or material inside a container.avoid guessing the contents
owner
Person with ownership of an item, requiring appropriate verification.verify the owner
claimant
Person stating that an item belongs to them.refer a claimant for verification
ownership claim
Statement that a person owns the item.verify an ownership claim
find location
Place where an item was discovered.record the find location
checkout
End-of-stay departure process for a guest.distinguish checkout from ownership
property record
Entry documenting a found item and relevant handling details.create a property record
item description
Identifying account of the item itself.record the item description
transfer
Passing an item to the designated next holder.document the transfer
receipt confirmation
Acknowledgement that the intended receiver has obtained the item.obtain receipt confirmation
custody
Responsibility for holding an item at a given stage.confirm custody
custody record
Record of who held or received an item through the process.maintain the custody record
reference number
Identifier attached to a record or case.record the reference number
privacy boundary
Limit on access to or disclosure of personal information.respect the privacy boundary
disclosure
Sharing of information with another person.avoid unauthorized disclosure
verified recipient
Person whose entitlement to receive the item has been established.identify the verified recipient
release
Authorized handover to the person entitled to receive the item.authorize release
site procedure
Required local process for handling the matter.follow the site procedure
factual correction
Amendment that replaces an inaccurate detail with the verified fact.make a factual correction

Glasses case describes the visible container. It does not prove glasses are inside. Keep the item closed in this scenario and transfer it to the designated receiver without turning a description into a search of private contents.

C Notice the language

Careful description protects everyone

A found item can prompt curiosity or pressure to identify its owner quickly. Keep the report neutral and limited to the visible details needed for the task. A confirmed transfer is useful progress without inventing ownership or discussing a guest with someone who has no verified role.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the report
I found a closed black glasses case.
Give the room
It was in room 214.
Give the position
It was beneath the chair.
State timing
I found it after checkout.
Preserve uncertainty
I do not know the owner.
Avoid a contents claim
I have not opened it, so I cannot describe what is inside.
Identify the receiver
Are you the designated lost-property receiver?
Request transfer
I need to hand this over through the site procedure.
Ask for receipt
Please confirm when you have received the item.
Repeat the description
Closed black glasses case, beneath the chair, room 214.
Separate custody and ownership
Receiving it does not identify its owner.
Protect privacy
I cannot share guest details with an unverified caller.
Route a claim
Ownership claims need the actual verification process.
Avoid release by guess
A room number alone is not release authorization.
Correct a number
The room is 214, not 241.
Close the handover
The transfer is recorded; ownership remains unverified.
6 language and precision notes
Found versus owned
Finding an item in a room does not prove who owns it.
Beneath
This gives a specific position rather than a vague room-level description.
Closed
Include this visible condition without asserting what the item contains.
Received versus released
Staff receipt and return to a verified owner are different handovers.
Unknown
Use unknown where no ownership evidence has been established.
After checkout
This describes when the discovery occurred, not who left the item.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which description is supported?

Answer and explanation

D. Closed black glasses case beneath the chair in room 214

The supported description stays within visible details and the stated location.

2. What does Dev's receipt establish?

Answer and explanation

B. Transfer to the designated receiver

Receipt confirms custody transfer, not ownership verification or final release.

3. Which statement exceeds the evidence?

Answer and explanation

B. It must belong to the last person who stayed in the room.

Room location and checkout timing do not establish the item's owner.

4. What should happen to a later claim?

Answer and explanation

B. Follow the actual ownership-verification and release process

A claim needs the designated process rather than an unsupported assumption or disclosure.

E Conversations

The closed black case

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ana: Room attendant / Dev: Designated lost-property receiver

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • ownership claim
  • visible description
  • receipt confirmation
  • lost-property receiver
  • property record
  • custody record
  • release
  • ownership
  • find location
  • privacy boundary
  1. Ana
    I have a found item from room two hundred fourteen, after checkout. Are you receiving lost property today?
  2. Dev
    I am the 1 for this handover. Please give me the visible description and exact find location before we record the transfer.
  3. Ana
    It is a closed black glasses case. I found it beneath the chair, and I have not opened it or inspected anything inside.
  4. Dev
    I will record that 2 without adding a contents description. A case designed for glasses does not prove what is actually inside it.
  5. Ana
    The room is two fourteen, not two forty-one. I found it beneath the chair.
  6. Dev
    I have the 3 as beneath the chair in room 214. I will read that back before finishing the entry.
  7. Ana
    That is right. It was found after checkout, but I do not know who owns it. I do not want to attach a guest name by guessing.
  8. Dev
    We will keep 4 unverified. The room and timing are useful facts, but they are not a completed identification of the person entitled to receive it.
  9. Ana
    Here is the closed case. Please confirm that you have received it for the record.
  10. Dev
    I have received the case. That is the 5 for this transfer. The record should show the real handover rather than an intention to pass it on later.
  11. Ana
    Thanks. I will record that I handed it to you, not that we returned it to a guest.
  12. Dev
    Exactly. The 6 describes this staff handover. Any later return is a separate event with its own verification and recording requirements.
  13. Ana
    If someone calls about it later, should I send that query to the designated lost-property process?
  14. Dev
    Yes. An 7 needs appropriate verification. We should not release an item simply because a caller supplies a room number or sounds confident.
  15. Ana
    And I should not discuss another guest's details while trying to help the caller. That would go beyond the information needed for this handover.
  16. Dev
    Correct. Keep the 8 clear and use the designated process. Helpful service does not require giving an unverified caller someone else's personal information.
  17. Ana
    Could you read back the item and location before I leave?
  18. Dev
    The 9 says closed black glasses case, found beneath the chair in room 214 after checkout, not opened by you, received by me.
  19. Ana
    That matches what happened. The staff transfer is complete, and we still do not have a verified owner or an authorized return to report.
  20. Dev
    Agreed. No 10 to a claimant has occurred. We have completed and recorded the custody transfer while leaving the unresolved ownership question accurately open.

Answers and explanations
  1. lost-property receiver Turn 2. Lost-property receiver identifies Dev's designated role in accepting the item through the site process, not ownership of it.
  2. visible description Turn 4. Visible description is limited to the closed black case and does not claim knowledge of its contents.
  3. find location Turn 6. Find location records room 214 and the position beneath the chair, helping identify the discovery accurately.
  4. ownership Turn 8. Ownership remains unverified; discovery in a room after checkout does not establish who owns the item.
  5. receipt confirmation Turn 10. Receipt confirmation establishes that Dev actually received the item, not merely that a transfer was proposed.
  6. custody record Turn 12. Custody record tracks who holds the item; a staff transfer does not mean it has been returned to a verified owner.
  7. ownership claim Turn 14. Ownership claim is a person's assertion, which must be verified through the actual process rather than accepted from a room number alone.
  8. privacy boundary Turn 16. Privacy boundary limits unnecessary or unauthorized disclosure of guest information during a found-property query.
  9. property record Turn 18. Property record captures the established item details and handling facts without inventing contents, ownership, or final release.
  10. release Turn 20. Release is the separate authorized return to an entitled recipient; the completed staff receipt does not establish it.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ana: I have a found item from room two hundred fourteen, after checkout. Are you receiving lost property today?
  2. Dev: I am the lost-property receiver for this handover. Please give me the visible description and exact find location before we record the transfer.
  3. Ana: It is a closed black glasses case. I found it beneath the chair, and I have not opened it or inspected anything inside.
  4. Dev: I will record that visible description without adding a contents description. A case designed for glasses does not prove what is actually inside it.
  5. Ana: The room is two fourteen, not two forty-one. I found it beneath the chair.
  6. Dev: I have the find location as beneath the chair in room 214. I will read that back before finishing the entry.
  7. Ana: That is right. It was found after checkout, but I do not know who owns it. I do not want to attach a guest name by guessing.
  8. Dev: We will keep ownership unverified. The room and timing are useful facts, but they are not a completed identification of the person entitled to receive it.
  9. Ana: Here is the closed case. Please confirm that you have received it for the record.
  10. Dev: I have received the case. That is the receipt confirmation for this transfer. The record should show the real handover rather than an intention to pass it on later.
  11. Ana: Thanks. I will record that I handed it to you, not that we returned it to a guest.
  12. Dev: Exactly. The custody record describes this staff handover. Any later return is a separate event with its own verification and recording requirements.
  13. Ana: If someone calls about it later, should I send that query to the designated lost-property process?
  14. Dev: Yes. An ownership claim needs appropriate verification. We should not release an item simply because a caller supplies a room number or sounds confident.
  15. Ana: And I should not discuss another guest's details while trying to help the caller. That would go beyond the information needed for this handover.
  16. Dev: Correct. Keep the privacy boundary clear and use the designated process. Helpful service does not require giving an unverified caller someone else's personal information.
  17. Ana: Could you read back the item and location before I leave?
  18. Dev: The property record says closed black glasses case, found beneath the chair in room 214 after checkout, not opened by you, received by me.
  19. Ana: That matches what happened. The staff transfer is complete, and we still do not have a verified owner or an authorized return to report.
  20. Dev: Agreed. No release to a claimant has occurred. We have completed and recorded the custody transfer while leaving the unresolved ownership question accurately open.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A case under the chair

The room 214 lost-property handover.

  1. Attendant: I found a closed black glasses case beneath the chair in room two-fourteen after checkout.
  2. Lost-property receiver: Have you opened it or identified the owner from anything visible on the outside?
  3. Attendant: I haven't opened it. My item description is based only on the outside.
  4. Lost-property receiver: I'll receive it through our lost-property arrangement. Please confirm the found location again.
  5. Attendant: Beneath the chair in two-fourteen, not on the desk or in the corridor.
  6. Lost-property receiver: I've received the closed black case; the custody record will show that found location.
  7. Attendant: A colleague assumed it belonged to the last guest, but that hasn't been confirmed.
  8. Lost-property receiver: We'll keep ownership unconfirmed. Any claimant can speak with the designated team about identification.
  9. Attendant: Do you need anything else from me to complete this transfer record?
  10. Lost-property receiver: Only your confirmation of the observation; I've accepted the item and the follow-up from here.
Papers beside a bin aren't necessarily waste

A commercial cleaner asks about loose documents.

  1. Cleaner: There are loose papers beside the recycling bin, but they aren't inside it.
  2. Office contact: Have they been marked for disposal, or are you asking because their status is unclear?
  3. Cleaner: Their status is unclear. I haven't read the contents or treated them as waste.
  4. Office contact: Thank you. Those belong to the meeting organizer, who left them for collection.
  5. Cleaner: I'll leave them in place and clean the accessible area around them.
  6. Office contact: Please do. The organizer will move them before your next scheduled visit.
  7. Cleaner: Should I record that this small floor area couldn't be completed because documents blocked access?
  8. Office contact: Yes, describe the blocked area without including any information from the papers themselves.
  9. Cleaner: I'll say the floor beside the recycling bin remains inaccessible due to stored documents.
  10. Office contact: That is enough. I'll arrange their removal without asking you to decide what can be discarded.
A visitor asks for someone else's property

A cleaner redirects an ownership claim to the designated team.

  1. Visitor: I heard you found a phone in the lounge. Can you give it to me?
  2. Cleaner: The lost-property team handles return requests. I can direct you to their desk.
  3. Visitor: It's probably my colleague's phone. They asked me to collect anything you found.
  4. Cleaner: I can't confirm ownership or release an item from that statement alone.
  5. Visitor: Could you at least tell me whose name appears on the screen?
  6. Cleaner: I haven't inspected private information. Please explain your request to the lost-property team.
  7. Visitor: Where is the desk, and will they know about the item from the lounge?
  8. Cleaner: The reception desk handles these inquiries and can check the relevant record.
  9. Visitor: I'll go there and provide the details they request to assess the claim.
  10. Cleaner: Thank you. They can discuss the return arrangement; I don't need your colleague's personal details here.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

REFERENCE
Course: Housekeeping and Commercial Cleaning English
Professional audience: Housekeeping attendants, commercial cleaners, custodial staff, and cleaning team members
Scope: All sites, rooms, quantities, guests, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not chemical handling, equipment operation, hazard control, legal advice, or specialist infection prevention. Follow actual training, product labels and safety data sheets, site access rules, privacy and found-property procedures, and emergency arrangements. No example authorizes unsafe access, use of an unidentified product, or release of an unchecked area.
Lesson 05: Handling found items and privacy questions
Original fictional case: After checkout, attendant Ana finds a closed black glasses case beneath the chair in room 214. The owner is unknown, and Ana does not open the case. Dev is the designated lost-property receiver and can accept it through the site procedure. The handover should record only established details and confirm receipt. A room number does not by itself identify the owner, and the appearance of a glasses case does not confirm what is inside. Any later ownership claim or release requires the actual verification process, not a guess during this conversation.
Language workshop: The closed black case
Communication goal: Describe found property without inspecting private contents, record its location, and confirm transfer to the designated receiver.
Language guidance: Found after checkout is a timing fact, not proof that the last guest owns the item. Record the room and location accurately while leaving ownership unknown until the actual process verifies it.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I found a closed black glasses case. / It was in room 214. / It was beneath the chair. / I found it after checkout. / I do not know the owner. / I have not opened it, so I cannot describe what is inside. / Are you the designated lost-property receiver? / I need to hand this over through the site procedure. / Please confirm when you have received the item. / Closed black glasses case, beneath the chair, room 214. / Receiving it does not identify its owner. / I cannot share guest details with an unverified caller. / Ownership claims need the actual verification process. / A room number alone is not release authorization. / The room is 214, not 241. / The transfer is recorded; ownership remains unverified.
Vocabulary: found property: Item discovered whose owner or custody needs appropriate handling. / lost-property receiver: Designated person who accepts found items under the site process. / visible description: Account limited to details observable without inspecting hidden contents. / glasses case: Container designed for spectacles, regardless of its actual contents. / closed item: Item whose interior is not exposed or inspected. / contents: Items or material inside a container. / owner: Person with ownership of an item, requiring appropriate verification. / claimant: Person stating that an item belongs to them. / ownership claim: Statement that a person owns the item. / find location: Place where an item was discovered. / checkout: End-of-stay departure process for a guest. / property record: Entry documenting a found item and relevant handling details. / item description: Identifying account of the item itself. / transfer: Passing an item to the designated next holder. / receipt confirmation: Acknowledgement that the intended receiver has obtained the item. / custody: Responsibility for holding an item at a given stage. / custody record: Record of who held or received an item through the process. / reference number: Identifier attached to a record or case. / privacy boundary: Limit on access to or disclosure of personal information. / disclosure: Sharing of information with another person. / verified recipient: Person whose entitlement to receive the item has been established. / release: Authorized handover to the person entitled to receive the item. / site procedure: Required local process for handling the matter. / factual correction: Amendment that replaces an inaccurate detail with the verified fact.
Speaking task: Read the property handover, saying 214 and 241 distinctly. Repeat the visible case description and where it was found without naming an owner. Complete the pouch record, distinguishing staff receipt from return to a claimant.
Partner: Ana: Room attendant / Dev: Designated lost-property receiver
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A closed navy pouch is found on the desk in room 318 after checkout. Its owner is unknown. Designated receiver Rosa confirms receiving it unopened.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ana: Could you read back the item and location before I leave?
Dev: The property record says closed black glasses case, found beneath the chair in room 214 after checkout, not opened by you, received by me.
Ana: That matches what happened. The staff transfer is complete, and we still do not have a verified owner or an authorized return to report.
Dev: Agreed. No release to a claimant has occurred. We have completed and recorded the custody transfer while leaving the unresolved ownership question accurately open.
END REFERENCE
06Counting linen and replenishing suppliesThree bath towels short+

Your goal

Report linen counts by item, calculate the exact shortfall, and separate expected delivery from stock on hand.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Three bath towels short

The third-floor cart target is twelve bath towels and eight hand towels. Attendant Noor counts nine bath towels and eight hand towels. A laundry delivery is expected but its arrival and contents are not confirmed. Supply lead Ben needs an item-specific report and a request for three additional bath towels, not a statement that all linen is unavailable. No delivery has been received or counted. The target is a cart-stock requirement, not a claim about the number of occupied rooms or a reason to substitute one towel type for another.

Noor: Room attendant / Ben: Supply lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the bath-towel shortfall?

Answer and explanation

A. Three

The target of twelve minus the counted nine leaves three bath towels.

2. What is the hand-towel status?

Answer and explanation

D. Eight counted against a target of eight

The hand-towel count already matches its specified target.

3. What is known about the delivery?

Answer and explanation

B. It is expected but not confirmed

Neither arrival nor contents are confirmed, so expected stock cannot close the shortfall.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Bath towels: target twelve, counted nine, short three. Hand towels: target eight, counted eight, no numerical shortfall. Keep item types separate even when giving a combined cart total.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
linen
Textile items used in housekeeping, such as towels and bedding.count the linen
bath towel
Larger towel intended for drying the body after bathing.replenish bath towels
hand towel
Smaller towel intended primarily for drying hands.count hand towels
bath mat
Textile or other item used on the floor near bathing facilities.identify the bath mat
pillowcase
Cover used over a pillow.replenish pillowcases
duvet cover
Removable fabric cover for a duvet.count duvet covers
flat sheet
Unfitted bed sheet without shaped elastic corners.identify a flat sheet
fitted sheet
Sheet designed to fit around a mattress.check fitted-sheet stock
linen cart
Mobile storage unit carrying linen supplies.replenish the linen cart
floor allocation
Supplies assigned to a particular building floor.check the floor allocation
stock target
Quantity specified as the desired amount on hand.confirm the stock target
par level
Planned stock level used for replenishment decisions.compare with par level
physical count
Quantity actually counted at the location.complete a physical count
stock on hand
Supplies currently present, not merely expected.report stock on hand
shortfall
Amount by which the current count is below the target.calculate the shortfall
item type
Category of supply that must remain distinct in the count.specify the item type
replenishment request
Request to restore the required stock.submit a replenishment request
laundry delivery
Transfer of linen from the laundry supply process.check the laundry delivery
expected delivery
Delivery anticipated but not yet confirmed as received.distinguish an expected delivery
confirmed receipt
Established arrival and acceptance under the relevant process.record confirmed receipt
delivery contents
Items and quantities actually included in a delivery.verify delivery contents
count discrepancy
Difference between a recorded or expected amount and a checked count.report a count discrepancy
substitution
Replacement of one item type with another under the required approval.avoid an unapproved substitution
stock update
Revised statement of actual inventory after a verified change.provide a stock update

Expected delivery is not stock on hand. The delivery may need arrival, contents, and other required checks before the cart record changes. Do not convert an anticipated delivery into a completed replenishment.

C Notice the language

Specific shortages are easier to solve

A broad statement such as we have no linen can cause unnecessary disruption. Name the cart, the towel type, the target, and the counted amount. Keep expected supplies out of the current count until the actual receipt and checks occur.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the cart
This is the third-floor linen cart.
State the target
The target is twelve bath towels.
Give the count
I counted nine bath towels.
Calculate the gap
We are three bath towels short.
Report the second item
Hand towels are eight against a target of eight.
Avoid overstatement
The shortage is not across all linen.
Request the exact amount
Please arrange three additional bath towels.
Preserve uncertainty
The laundry delivery is expected but not confirmed.
Keep future stock separate
I have not added expected items to the current count.
Ask about receipt
Has the delivery actually arrived and been checked?
Clarify contents
Do we know what the delivery contains?
Reject a silent substitute
Hand towels are not an assumed replacement for bath towels.
Update after verification
I will revise the count after the actual receipt and checks.
Keep the unit visible
Three bath towels, not three carts or packs.
Name the remaining need
The bath-towel shortfall remains open.
Close the count report
Nine bath towels and eight hand towels are currently counted.
6 language and precision notes
Against
Nine against twelve compares actual stock with the target.
Short by
Short by three describes the missing amount, not the amount present.
On hand
This means actually available in the stated location.
Expected
Expected is weaker than arrived, received, or counted.
Additional
Three additional bath towels would fill the numerical gap if actually received and accepted.
By item
Separate categories prevent a correct total from hiding the wrong mix.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report is best?

Answer and explanation

A. Nine of twelve bath towels; eight of eight hand towels; three bath towels short.

The report preserves each item count, target, and exact numerical shortage.

2. Why not count the expected delivery?

Answer and explanation

C. Its arrival and contents are unconfirmed.

Anticipation does not establish that the needed items are present and checked.

3. Which request matches the shortfall?

Answer and explanation

C. Three additional bath towels

Three bath towels fill the difference between the target and current count.

4. What does the cart target establish?

Answer and explanation

D. A required stock mix, not the number of occupied rooms

The target specifies cart quantities and does not supply occupancy or substitution authority.

E Conversations

Three bath towels short

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Noor: Room attendant / Ben: Supply lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • expected delivery
  • stock target
  • linen cart
  • replenishment request
  • substitution
  • delivery contents
  • Stock on hand
  • stock update
  • physical count
  • shortfall
  1. Noor
    I have counted the third-floor linen cart. The bath towels are below target, but the hand towels match their target.
  2. Ben
    Please give me the 1 and the target for each type. I want the item-specific figures before arranging replenishment.
  3. Noor
    The target is twelve bath towels, and I counted nine. For hand towels, the target is eight and the count is also eight.
  4. Ben
    That gives a 2 of three bath towels. The hand towels have no numerical gap against their target, so the two categories need different status messages.
  5. Noor
    Yes, just the bath towels are short. We do have the full eight hand towels.
  6. Ben
    We should name the 3 and the two item types. This is the third-floor cart report, not a claim about every cupboard or every floor.
  7. Noor
    Laundry is expected today, but I have not received or counted anything from that delivery yet.
  8. Ben
    Then it remains an 4. We cannot use it to close the bath-towel shortage until the actual supply and relevant checks are established.
  9. Noor
    I have kept it out of the current count. I do not want to report twelve just because a delivery may be on the way.
  10. Ben
    Correct. 5 remains nine bath towels and eight hand towels. An expected arrival is useful planning information, but it is not counted stock.
  11. Noor
    Please request three individual bath towels for this cart, not three packs.
  12. Ben
    I will make the 6 for three additional bath towels. Keeping the unit explicit prevents us from ordering or recording an unintended quantity.
  13. Noor
    The eight hand towels are already allocated to their own target. I have not offered them as substitutes.
  14. Ben
    No 7 has been approved. A hand towel and a bath towel are different stock items, and the present count does not authorize changing the required mix.
  15. Noor
    Understood. When the delivery does arrive, I will need to check what it actually includes rather than assume it contains the missing bath towels.
  16. Ben
    Yes, verify the 8 through the normal process. An arrival by itself does not establish that the three needed bath towels are in it.
  17. Noor
    I will update you after receipt and checking. Until then, the bath-towel count stays at nine.
  18. Ben
    That is when the 9 should change the recorded figures. We should preserve the earlier count rather than retroactively pretend the cart was already full.
  19. Noor
    My final read-back is third-floor cart: nine bath towels against twelve, eight hand towels against eight, with three bath towels still needed.
  20. Ben
    Agreed. The 10 remains twelve and eight respectively. The delivery is expected, not confirmed, and the request is for three additional bath towels.

Answers and explanations
  1. physical count Turn 2. Physical count is the quantity actually observed on the cart, which must remain separate from expected delivery stock.
  2. shortfall Turn 4. Shortfall is the difference between the bath-towel target of twelve and the actual count of nine.
  3. linen cart Turn 6. Linen cart locates this count on the third floor; the report does not establish inventory for the entire building.
  4. expected delivery Turn 8. Expected delivery describes anticipated supply, not confirmed arrival, checked contents, or completed replenishment.
  5. Stock on hand Turn 10. Stock on hand is the actual supply present now; future or unconfirmed items must not be included.
  6. replenishment request Turn 12. Replenishment request specifies three additional bath towels and preserves the individual-item unit needed to fill the gap.
  7. substitution Turn 14. Substitution would replace one item type with another; the count supplies no approval to use hand towels for the bath-towel shortage.
  8. delivery contents Turn 16. Delivery contents identify the actual items and quantities supplied; an arrival alone does not prove the required bath towels are included.
  9. stock update Turn 18. Stock update should reflect a verified change in actual quantities, not a prediction about an unconfirmed delivery.
  10. stock target Turn 20. Stock target specifies the required cart mix; it is not evidence about occupied rooms or completed replenishment.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Noor: I have counted the third-floor linen cart. The bath towels are below target, but the hand towels match their target.
  2. Ben: Please give me the physical count and the target for each type. I want the item-specific figures before arranging replenishment.
  3. Noor: The target is twelve bath towels, and I counted nine. For hand towels, the target is eight and the count is also eight.
  4. Ben: That gives a shortfall of three bath towels. The hand towels have no numerical gap against their target, so the two categories need different status messages.
  5. Noor: Yes, just the bath towels are short. We do have the full eight hand towels.
  6. Ben: We should name the linen cart and the two item types. This is the third-floor cart report, not a claim about every cupboard or every floor.
  7. Noor: Laundry is expected today, but I have not received or counted anything from that delivery yet.
  8. Ben: Then it remains an expected delivery. We cannot use it to close the bath-towel shortage until the actual supply and relevant checks are established.
  9. Noor: I have kept it out of the current count. I do not want to report twelve just because a delivery may be on the way.
  10. Ben: Correct. Stock on hand remains nine bath towels and eight hand towels. An expected arrival is useful planning information, but it is not counted stock.
  11. Noor: Please request three individual bath towels for this cart, not three packs.
  12. Ben: I will make the replenishment request for three additional bath towels. Keeping the unit explicit prevents us from ordering or recording an unintended quantity.
  13. Noor: The eight hand towels are already allocated to their own target. I have not offered them as substitutes.
  14. Ben: No substitution has been approved. A hand towel and a bath towel are different stock items, and the present count does not authorize changing the required mix.
  15. Noor: Understood. When the delivery does arrive, I will need to check what it actually includes rather than assume it contains the missing bath towels.
  16. Ben: Yes, verify the delivery contents through the normal process. An arrival by itself does not establish that the three needed bath towels are in it.
  17. Noor: I will update you after receipt and checking. Until then, the bath-towel count stays at nine.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Only the bath towels are short

The third-floor cart count from the case.

  1. Attendant: I've counted nine bath towels and eight hand towels on the third-floor cart.
  2. Linen colleague: Our par stock is twelve bath towels and eight hand towels. What's the shortfall?
  3. Attendant: Three bath towels. The hand-towel count is complete, and I haven't reported a general linen stockout.
  4. Linen colleague: A laundry delivery is expected this afternoon, but I don't have a confirmed arrival time.
  5. Attendant: Could you check whether three bath towels can be allocated from stock already available?
  6. Linen colleague: I'll check that now. Are the nine on your cart all assigned to this floor?
  7. Attendant: Yes, none are reserved for another floor or already promised for a separate request.
  8. Linen colleague: Then I'll look for the three-item shortfall rather than send you another full cart allocation.
  9. Attendant: Thank you. Please tell me if the request is accepted or still waiting on delivery.
  10. Linen colleague: I found three available bath towels and can allocate them now; laundry timing remains separate.
A carton count hides the refill type

A cleaner checks supplies delivered to an office washroom.

  1. Cleaner: Two cartons arrived, but only one has the paper-towel refill type listed for our washroom.
  2. Stores colleague: What's in the other carton? Does its label identify a different dispenser refill?
  3. Cleaner: Yes, it is labeled for the larger dispenser used on the ground floor.
  4. Stores colleague: Then two cartons delivered doesn't mean two suitable cartons for your assigned washroom.
  5. Cleaner: Exactly. I can report one correct carton and one allocation that needs checking.
  6. Stores colleague: I'll review the delivery destination rather than ask you to make the wrong refill fit.
  7. Cleaner: The upper-floor dispensers still need the listed type. Can you check available stock?
  8. Stores colleague: Yes. I'll confirm the correct product code and the number available before promising replenishment.
  9. Cleaner: I'll keep the upper-floor shortage separate from the total number of cartons received.
  10. Stores colleague: That will make the request clear and prevent another delivery of the wrong refill type.
An allocation isn't extra stock

A room attendant checks whether linen in another cart is available.

  1. Attendant: The neighboring cart has six spare-looking sheets. Can I take two for my assignment?
  2. Team lead: They may already be allocated. Which room tasks are you short on right now?
  3. Attendant: I'm short two sheets against my current assignment, and my own cart has none remaining.
  4. Team lead: Thanks. I'll check that cart's allocation before assuming its visible supplies are spare.
  5. Attendant: I don't want to leave the next attendant short just because their cart looks full.
  6. Team lead: Exactly. The count is six, but the room list shows all six assigned to their floor.
  7. Attendant: Then I need another source for my two, rather than reclassify those sheets as unassigned.
  8. Team lead: I'll check the linen room for available stock and tell you the confirmed amount.
  9. Attendant: Please also confirm whether that changes my room sequence while we wait for supplies.
  10. Team lead: Yes. You can take the supply-independent tasks first; we'll keep the two-sheet shortfall visible.
